On an angle-averaged Neumann-to-Dirichlet map for thin filaments
Laurel Ohm

Abstract
. We consider the Laplace equation in the exterior of a thin filament in ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} and perform a detailed decomposition of a notion of slender body Neumann-to-Dirichlet (NtD) and Dirichlet-to-Neumann (DtN) maps along the filament surface. The decomposition is motivated by a filament evolution equation in Stokes flow for which the Laplace setting serves as an important toy problem. Given a general curved, closed filament with constant radius ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 , we show that both the slender body DtN and NtD maps may be decomposed into the corresponding operator about a straight, periodic filament plus lower order remainders. For the straight filament, both the slender body NtD and DtN maps are given by explicit Fourier multipliers and it is straightforward to compute their mapping properties. The remainder terms are lower order in the sense that they are small with respect to ϵ \epsilon or smoother. While the strategy here is meant to serve as a blueprint for the Stokes setting, the Laplace problem may be of independent interest.



1.
Introduction

We consider the Laplace equation in the exterior of a thin filament in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and study a
slender body Neumann-to-Dirichlet (NtD) map
and its inverse, the slender body Dirichlet-to-Neumann (DtN) map, along the filament surface. The boundary value problem corresponding to the slender body NtD map was introduced by the author, together with Mori and Spirn, in
[
36
,
37
]
in the context of the Stokes equations as mathematical justification for
slender body theory
, an approximation for the interaction between a thin filament and a 3D viscous fluid. Slender body theory, developed and improved in various classical works
[
5
,
11
,
20
,
21
,
23
,
25
,
30
,
45
]
, approximates the immersed filament as a 1D curve evolving according to a 1D force-to-velocity map along the fiber centerline. Making rigorous sense of this 1D force-to-velocity map for a truly 3D filament motivated the development of the slender body NtD map in
[
36
,
37
]
for a static filament, i.e., a fixed filament geometry. The PDE results in this paper are motivated by a filament evolution problem in the Stokes setting using the slender body NtD map. Here we study the Laplace version of the slender body NtD map as an important toy model for the Stokes setting.

The main result of this paper is Theorem
1.2
, which provides a detailed decomposition of the Laplace slender body NtD and DtN maps about a general curved, closed filament with constant radius
\epsilon>0
. We show that for both operators, the leading order mapping properties are given by the corresponding operator about a straight, periodic filament with radius
\epsilon
. In this simple geometry, the slender body NtD and DtN maps are both given by explicit Fourier multipliers, and it is relatively straightforward to compute their mapping properties. We extract the straight operator from the expression for the general curved filament and show that the remainder terms are lower order in the sense that they are small with respect to
\epsilon
or smoother. The arguments presented here are meant to serve as a blueprint for performing the same type of decomposition in the Stokes setting.

Main motivation:
Our long-term aim is to develop a complete well-posedness theory for the evolution equation (
1
) describing the dynamics of an immersed elastic filament in Stokes flow. This would provide a more complete mathematical justification of the myriad computational results for thin filament dynamics based on slender body theory. Let
\bm{X}(s,t)
denote the centerline position of a filament immersed in 3D Stokes flow at time
t
. We consider the evolution of
\bm{X}
according to

(1)

\frac{\partial\bm{X}}{\partial t}=-({\rm SB\,NtD})\big[(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s})_{s}\big]\,,\qquad\left\lvert\bm{X}_{s}\right\rvert=1\,.

Here (SB NtD) denotes a version of Neumann-to-Dirichlet map in 3D Stokes flow taking force data defined along a 1D curve to the motion of the filament centerline. The way in which we propose to make sense of this map when the filament is interacting with a 3D fluid will be made more precise later (in the context of the Laplace equation–see equation (
8
)).
The form of the force data
(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s})_{s}
comes from Euler-Bernoulli beam theory
[
7
,
8
,
22
,
45
,
46
,
47
]
and is a simple elastic response along the filament with
\lambda(s,t)
, the filament tension, serving as a Lagrange multiplier to enforce the inextensibility constraint
\left\lvert\bm{X}_{s}\right\rvert=1
.
The evolution (
1
) bears analogies to the Peskin problem for a 1D immersed elastic filament in 2D
[
9
,
10
,
17
,
18
,
31
,
28
,
38
,
43
,
44
]
, but here the filament is immersed in a 3D fluid and part of the difficulty in analyzing this problem is simply making sense of the operator (SB NtD) along the filament.

The PDE evolution (
1
) is studied in
[
35
,
40
]
when (SB NtD) is replaced with
local
slender body theory
(\text{SB}_{\rm loc}\,\text{NtD})
, given simply by multiplication by the matrix
c\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert({\bf I}+\bm{X}_{s}\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm T})
. In this setting, in addition to establishing well-posedness for the analogue of (
1
), we show how the addition of a time-periodic forcing can give rise to swimming, i.e., net forward motion. However, this map incorporates only a very simplified description of the effects of the surrounding fluid on the filament. In particular, this is not yet a truly coupled fluid-structure free boundary problem.
To incorporate more of the physics of the fluid-structure interaction, one can try using
nonlocal
slender body theory
(\text{SB}_{\rm nloc}\,\text{NtD})

[
25
,
23
,
45
]
, which is a 1D integral operator incorporating nonlocal interactions along the length of the filament. However, from a PDE perspective, this 1D integral operator cannot yield a well-posed evolution problem in the form (
1
) due to issues at high wavenumbers leading to nonsensical mapping properties
[
20
,
34
,
45
,
41
]
. It is a nontrivial issue to incorporate more of the fluid physics while yielding a well-posed PDE. The challenge here is to make sense of a map from 1D force data to a 1D filament velocity when the filament is in 3D.

In
[
36
,
37
]
, we developed a notion of the operator (SB NtD) as a novel type of boundary value problem in the exterior of a filament with small but finite radius. In our setup, at each instant in time, the total force density
\bm{f}(s)
over each 3D cross section of the filament is prescribed, and we solve for the corresponding filament velocity
\bm{u}(s)
, unknown but constrained to be constant on cross sections, as a Stokes boundary value problem (compare with equation (
8
) in the Laplace setting).
The resulting boundary value problem is nonstandard but well posed in a natural energy space
[
36
,
37
,
33
]
. Crucially, this formulation also incorporates much more of the surrounding fluid physics than local slender body theory does. With this operator, (
1
) is a true free boundary problem.

To develop the well-posedness theory of (
1
) for the full (SB NtD) operator, we need a detailed understanding of its mapping properties. In particular, given the form
(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s})_{s}
of the forcing terms, we need to understand how this operator interacts with derivatives along the filament.
Here we perform a full decomposition of the analogous (SB NtD) operator in the Laplace setting, providing an important stepping stone towards tackling the Stokes version. In particular, all of the major tools used here have an analogue in the Stokes setting. The analysis is already technical in the simpler Laplace setting, which underscores why such a blueprint is useful.

Our strategy is to consider the general curved, closed filament as a perturbation of the straight filament. This strategy has been used to study vortex filament solutions of the Ginzburg-Landau, Navier-Stokes, and Euler equations
[
6
,
12
,
13
]
,
and bears analogy to the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator decompositions used to study the Muskat problem
[
2
,
15
,
39
]
and water waves
[
1
,
3
,
29
]
, where the strategy is to perturb around a flat interface.
Extracting the straight operator as the leading order behavior is useful since it is given by an explicit Fourier multiplier, computed in
[
34
]
. In particular, we have a Fourier multiplier representation for both the Dirichlet-to-Neumann and Neumann-to-Dirichlet directions, and we know exactly how this operator interacts with derivatives along the filament.

Additional motivation:

Despite no clear analogue of the full dynamic problem (
1
) for Laplace, the Laplace slender body DtN and NtD maps have many conceivable applications for which a detailed understanding of mapping properties may be useful. Examples include modeling blood or chemical perfusion in tissue outside of a thin capillary using Darcy flow (see, e.g.,
[
27
]
), describing the electrostatic potential outside of a conducting wire, and computing the equilibrium temperature distribution outside of a heating wire. In many of these cases, the full power of the decomposition performed here is likely not necessary to completely understand the model behavior. Nevertheless, this paper provides a foundational result for further work on problems involving the Laplace equation outside a thin domain.

1.1.
Geometry

Let
\bm{X}:\mathbb{T}\equiv\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
denote the coordinates of a closed curve
\Gamma_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, parameterized by arclength
s
(see figure
1
). We will require
\bm{X}(s)
to belong to the Hölder space
C^{2,\beta}(\mathbb{T})
(see (
11
)) and to satisfy the non-self-intersection condition

(2)

\inf_{s\neq s^{\prime}}\frac{\left\lvert\bm{X}(s)-\bm{X}(s^{\prime})\right\rvert}{\left\lvert s-s^{\prime}\right\rvert}\geq c_{\Gamma}

for some constant
c_{\Gamma}>0
. Letting
\bm{e}_{\rm t}(s):=\frac{\partial\bm{X}}{\partial s}
denote the unit tangent vector to
\bm{X}(s)
, we parameterize points sufficiently close to
\Gamma_{0}
using the
C^{1,\beta}
orthonormal frame
(\bm{e}_{\rm t}(s),\bm{e}_{\rm n_{1}}(s),\bm{e}_{\rm n_{2}}(s))
satisfying the ODEs

(3)

\frac{d}{ds}\begin{pmatrix}\bm{e}_{\rm t}(s)\\
\bm{e}_{\rm n_{1}}(s)\\
\bm{e}_{\rm n_{2}}(s)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\kappa_{1}(s)&\kappa_{2}(s)\\
-\kappa_{1}(s)&0&\kappa_{3}\\
-\kappa_{2}(s)&-\kappa_{3}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\bm{e}_{\rm t}(s)\\
\bm{e}_{\rm n_{1}}(s)\\
\bm{e}_{\rm n_{2}}(s)\end{pmatrix}\,.

Here the coefficients
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
satisfy

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}^{2}(s)+\kappa_{2}^{2}(s)=\kappa^{2}(s)\,,

where
\kappa(s)
is the curvature of
\bm{X}(s)
, and
\kappa_{3}
is a constant satisfying
\left\lvert\kappa_{3}\right\rvert\leq\pi
(see
[
36
, Lemma 1.1]
). We denote

(4)

\kappa_{*}:=\max_{s\in\mathbb{T}}\left\lvert\kappa(s)\right\rvert,\quad\kappa_{*,\beta}:=\max_{s\in\mathbb{T}}\frac{\left\lvert\kappa(s)-\kappa(s^{\prime})\right\rvert}{\left\lvert s-s^{\prime}\right\rvert^{\beta}}\,.

It will be useful to define the following curved version of cylindrical basis vectors:

(5)

\bm{e}_{r}(s,\theta)=\cos\theta\bm{e}_{\rm n_{1}}(s)+\sin\theta\bm{e}_{\rm n_{2}}(s)\,,\quad\bm{e}_{\theta}(s,\theta)=-\sin\theta\bm{e}_{\rm n_{1}}(s)+\cos\theta\bm{e}_{\rm n_{2}}(s)\,.

Within a neighborhood
{\rm dist}(\bm{x},\Gamma_{0})<r_{*}(c_{\Gamma},\kappa_{*})<\frac{1}{2\kappa_{*}}
of
\Gamma_{0}
, we may parameterize points
\bm{x}
as

\displaystyle\bm{x}=\bm{X}(s)+r\bm{e}_{r}(s,\theta),\quad 0\leq r<r_{*}\,.

For
\epsilon<r_{*}/4
, we define a slender filament of uniform radius
\epsilon
as

(6)

\Sigma_{\epsilon}:=\big\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\;:\;\bm{x}=\bm{X}(s)+r\bm{e}_{r}(s,\theta)\,,\;s\in\mathbb{T}\,,\;r<\epsilon\,,\;\theta\in 2\pi\mathbb{T}\big\}\,.

We define the filament surface
\Gamma_{\epsilon}=\partial\Sigma_{\epsilon}
as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\epsilon}:=\big\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\;:\;\bm{x}=\bm{X}(s)+\epsilon\bm{e}_{r}(s,\theta)\,,\;s\in\mathbb{T}\,,\;\theta\in 2\pi\mathbb{T}\big\}

and parameterize the surface element
\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}(s,\theta)
along the filament surface as

(7)

\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}(s,\theta)=\epsilon(1-\epsilon\widehat{\kappa}(s,\theta))\,,\qquad\widehat{\kappa}(s,\theta):=\kappa_{1}(s)\cos\theta+\kappa_{2}(s)\sin\theta\,.

Figure 1
.
An example of curved filament
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
described in section
1.1
and the straight filament
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
described in (
12
).

1.2.
Slender body Neumann-to-Dirichlet map

We need to make sense of a map between 1D Neumann data and 1D Dirichlet data in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. For the Laplace equation, this map will be defined via the following
slender body boundary value problem
:

(8)

\displaystyle\Delta u

\displaystyle=0\qquad\qquad\text{in }\Omega_{\epsilon}=\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\overline{\Sigma_{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\bm{n}_{x}}\,\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}(s,\theta)\,d\theta

\displaystyle=f(s)\qquad\,\;\text{on }\Gamma_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle u\big|_{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle=v(s)\,,\qquad\text{independent of }\theta\,.

In the “forward” version of the problem, we prescribe the
total
Neumann data
f(s)
per cross section of the filament surface, and we seek the unique function
u
which is harmonic in
\Omega_{\epsilon}=\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\overline{\Sigma_{\epsilon}}
and constant over every cross section of
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
, i.e. a function of arclength
s
only (see figure
1
).
In the “inverse” version of the problem, we prescribe Dirichlet data
v(s)
that is independent of the cross sectional angle parameter
\theta
and solve for
u
in
\Omega_{\epsilon}
and, subsequently, the integral of
\frac{\partial u}{\partial\bm{n}_{x}}
over each cross section.

Our analysis centers on the relationship between the total Neumann boundary data
f(s)
and the
\theta
-independent Dirichlet data
v(s)
. Our aim is to understand the
slender body Neumann-to-Dirichlet (NtD) map

(9)

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}:f(s)\mapsto v(s)

as well as its inverse, the
slender body Dirichlet-to-Neumann (DtN) map

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}:v(s)\mapsto f(s)
. These maps are the slender body analogues of the standard Neumann-to-Dirichlet and Dirichlet-to-Neumann maps for harmonic functions, defined only between functions which depend solely on the arclength parameter
s
along
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
.

The slender body inverse problem is simpler than the forward problem, as it is just a Dirichlet problem with
\theta
-independent data, and classical regularity results apply. Our strategy for developing the regularity theory of the trickier forward problem in many ways exploits the fact that the inverse problem is simpler to study. The forward problem was introduced in the Stokes setting in
[
36
,
37
]
and well-posedness was established in a natural energy space. Here we develop the regularity theory for the forward problem in the setting of Hölder spaces (see Lemma
1.8
and its proof in section
5
), since this will be a convenient setting for extracting detailed mapping information from the layer potential formulation of (
8
) considered in section
1.2.3
.

For
h
defined along
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
, we denote

(10)

\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}=\sup_{\bm{x}\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}}\left\lvert h(\bm{x})\right\rvert\,,\qquad\left\lvert h\right\rvert_{\dot{C}^{0,\alpha}}=\sup_{\bm{x}\neq\bm{x}^{\prime}}\frac{\left\lvert h(\bm{x})-h(\bm{x}^{\prime})\right\rvert}{\left\lvert\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\right\rvert^{\alpha}}\,,\quad 0<\alpha<1\,,

and recall the definition of the Hölder spaces
C^{k,\alpha}
along
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
as

(11)

C^{k,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})=\{h\;:\;\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{C^{k,\alpha}}<\infty\}\,,\qquad\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{C^{k,\alpha}}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\left\lVert\nabla^{\ell}h\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\left\lvert\nabla^{k}h\right\rvert_{\dot{C}^{0,\alpha}}\,.

1.2.1.
Slender body NtD for the straight filament

In the simple geometry of a straight filament, an explicit Fourier multiplier representation of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
was derived in
[
34
]
.
This explicit information will play a major role in our analysis of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
in the more general curved setting.

In the case of the straight cylinder, the filament centerline
\bm{X}(s)
may be written
\bm{X}(s)=s\bm{e}_{z}
,
s\in\mathbb{T}
, where
\bm{e}_{z}
is the standard Cartesian basis vector (see figure
1
). The vectors
\bm{e}_{r}(s,\theta)
and
\bm{e}_{\theta}(s,\theta)
(
5
) reduce to the usual cylindrical basis vectors
\bm{e}_{r}(\theta)
,
\bm{e}_{\theta}(\theta)
. We will use
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
to denote the surface
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
in the straight setting, i.e.

(12)

\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}:=\big\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}\;:\;\bm{x}=s\bm{e}_{z}+\epsilon\bm{e}_{r}(\theta)\,,\;s\in\mathbb{T}\,,\;\theta\in 2\pi\mathbb{T}\big\}\,.

Letting
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
,
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
denote the slender body NtD and DtN maps along
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
, from
[
34
]
we have that the symbol of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
on the unit circle
\mathbb{T}
is given by

(13)

m_{\epsilon}^{-1}(k)=4\pi^{2}\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert\frac{K_{1}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)}{K_{0}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)}\,,\quad\left\lvert k\right\rvert=1,2,3,\dots\,,

where
K_{0}
and
K_{1}
denote zeroth and first order modified Bessel functions of the second kind. In
[
34
]
, it is shown that at low wavenumbers
k\lesssim\frac{1}{\epsilon}
, the growth of
m_{\epsilon}^{-1}(k)
is logarithmic:

(14)

m_{\epsilon}^{-1}(k)\sim\left\lvert\log(\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\right\rvert^{-1}\,,\qquad k\lesssim\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,,

while
m_{\epsilon}^{-1}(k)
grows linearly as
\left\lvert k\right\rvert\to\infty
:

(15)

4\pi^{2}\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert\leq m_{\epsilon}^{-1}(k)\leq 4\pi^{2}\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert+2\pi\qquad\text{for all }k\,.

From (
15
), the
L^{2}
mapping properties of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
are clear:
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
maps the Sobolev space
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
to
H^{s+1}(\mathbb{T})
, and vice versa for
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
.
In addition, from the expression (
13
), we can obtain the following Hölder space mapping properties along
\mathbb{T}
.

Lemma 1.1

(Mapping properties of SB NtD and DtN on
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
)
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
denote the slender body NtD map along the straight filament
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
. Given
h\in C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
with
\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(s)\,ds=0
, we have

(16)

\left\lVert\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}[h]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\leq c\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{1/2}\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}+c\,\epsilon^{-1}\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{1/2}|h|_{\dot{C}^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

Similarly, let
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
denote the slender body DtN map along
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
. Given
g\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
, we have

(17)

\left\lVert\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}[g]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\leq c\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{-1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

The proof of Lemma
1.1
appears in section
2
.

1.2.2.
Statement of main theorem

Given that we have explicit Fourier multiplier expressions for
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
in the straight setting, the goal of this paper is to show that this explicit information in fact provides the leading order behavior for more general curved filaments. Using a layer potential representation of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
for a general closed filament as in section
1.1
, we extract the straight operator
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
and show that the remainders are lower order with respect to regularity or size in
\epsilon
. The decomposition of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
is then used to show that
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
may similarly be decomposed as the straight operator
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
plus lower order remainders.

Theorem 1.2

(Decomposition of slender body DtN and NtD)
.

Let
0<\alpha<\gamma<\beta<1
. Given a closed filament
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
as in section
1.1
with centerline
\bm{X}(s)\in C^{2,\beta}
, the slender body Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
may be decomposed as

(18)

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}=\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}+\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon}+\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}

where, given
v(s)\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
, we have

(19)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon}[v]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{2-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}}\,c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}[v]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{+}}},c_{\Gamma},\epsilon)\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

for any
\alpha^{+}\in(\alpha,\beta]
and any
\gamma^{+}\in(\gamma,\beta]
.

For
\epsilon
sufficiently small we may similarly decompose the slender body Neumann-to-Dirichlet operator
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
defined in (
9
) as

(20)

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}=({\bf I}+\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,\epsilon})\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}+\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,+}

where, given
f(s)\in C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
with
\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(s)\,ds=0
, we have

(21)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,\epsilon}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}[f]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{1-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}}\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{1/2}\,c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}[f]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,+}[f]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\kappa_{*,\gamma^{+}}},c_{\Gamma},\epsilon)\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

for any
\alpha^{+}\in(\alpha,\beta]
and any
\gamma^{+}\in(\gamma,\beta]
.

Remark
.

The goal of this work is to establish a direct route for extracting the necessary information for addressing this difficult physical problem (
1
). Here we use a boundary integral representation of the DtN map to explicitly extract the straight filament symbol as the leading order behavior. In principle, it should be possible to frame the analysis of the slender body NtD and DtN in terms of pseudodifferential calculus to obtain more general results for these maps. Here, however, we opt for directness over full generality.

The proof of Theorem
1.2
relies on a collection of key lemmas, stated below (Lemmas
1.3
–
1.8
).
We will immediately use these key lemmas to prove Theorem
1.2
in section
1.3
. The remainder of the paper will then be devoted to proving each key lemma.

1.2.3.
Layer potential formulation of slender body DtN map

To understand the mapping properties of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
along the more general curved filaments described in section
1.1
, we will rely on a representation of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
in terms of layer potentials on
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
. This representation is inspired by but differs from the boundary integral formulation of the Stokes slender body PDE proposed for numerical purposes in
[
32
]
. From this representation, we will then extract the straight operator
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
as the leading order behavior. In particular, the remainder terms arising due to filament curvature can be shown to be lower order with respect to regularity or with respect to size in terms of the filament radius
\epsilon
. Here and throughout, we will use overline notation
\overline{(\cdot)}
to denote functions defined along the straight filament
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
.

We begin by defining notation and recalling some results from potential theory (see, e.g.
[
16
,
26
,
42
]
).
Let
\mathcal{G}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})
denote the fundamental solution of the Laplace equation in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
:

(22)

\mathcal{G}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{1}{\left\lvert\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\right\rvert}\,.

For
\bm{x}^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}
, we define

(23)

K_{\mathcal{D}}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})=\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})}{\partial\bm{n}_{x^{\prime}}}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{(\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime})\cdot\bm{n}_{x^{\prime}}}{\left\lvert\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\right\rvert^{3}}\,,

where
\bm{n}_{x^{\prime}}
is the unit normal vector to
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
at
\bm{x}^{\prime}
pointing
out from
the slender body
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
.
Given a continuous function
\varphi(\bm{x}^{\prime})
defined along the filament surface
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
, for
\bm{x}\in\Omega_{\epsilon}
we define the single layer potential
\mathcal{S}
and double layer potential
\mathcal{D}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}[\varphi](\bm{x})=\int_{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}\mathcal{G}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})\varphi(\bm{x}^{\prime})\,dS_{x^{\prime}}\,,\qquad\mathcal{D}[\varphi](\bm{x})=\int_{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}K_{\mathcal{D}}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})\varphi(\bm{x}^{\prime})\,dS_{x^{\prime}}\,,

where
dS_{x^{\prime}}
denotes the surface element with respect to
\bm{x}^{\prime}
on
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
. For
\bm{x}\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}
, the single layer potential is continuous up to
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
, while the double layer potential satisfies the exterior jump relation

(24)

\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{D}[\varphi](\bm{x}+h\bm{n}_{x})=\mathcal{D}[\varphi](\bm{x})+\frac{1}{2}\varphi(\bm{x})\,,

Here again
\bm{n}_{x}
is the unit normal vector at
\bm{x}\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}
exterior to the slender body
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
(see
[
26
]
, Theorems 6.15 and 6.18).

Now, given sufficiently smooth functions
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
in
\Omega_{\epsilon}
with sufficiently fast decay at infinity, letting
v_{1}=u_{1}\big|_{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}
and
v_{2}=u_{2}\big|_{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}
, we have Green’s formula:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\epsilon}}\bigg(u_{1}\Delta u_{2}-u_{2}\Delta u_{1}\bigg)\,d\bm{x}=-\int_{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}\bigg(v_{1}\frac{\partial u_{2}}{\partial\bm{n}_{x}}-v_{2}\frac{\partial u_{1}}{\partial\bm{n}_{x}}\bigg)\,dS_{x},\quad\bm{n}_{x}\text{ exterior normal to }\Sigma_{\epsilon}\,.

This follows by applying the divergence theorem to the vector field
u_{1}\nabla u_{2}-u_{2}\nabla u_{1}
in
\Omega_{\epsilon}
. Note that the minus sign on the right hand side arises because
\bm{n}_{x}
points out of the slender body
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
, so it is really the interior normal vector to
\Omega_{\epsilon}
. If
u_{1}
is harmonic, then, fixing
\bm{x}_{0}\in\Omega_{\epsilon}
and taking
u_{2}(\bm{x})=\mathcal{G}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x})
, and noting
\Delta_{x}u_{2}=-\delta(\bm{x}-\bm{x}_{0})
, we obtain

(25)

\displaystyle u_{1}(\bm{x}_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}[w_{1}](\bm{x}_{0})+\mathcal{D}[v_{1}](\bm{x}_{0});

\displaystyle w_{1}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle:=-\frac{\partial u_{1}}{\partial\bm{n}_{x}}\bigg|_{\Gamma_{\epsilon}},\quad\bm{n}_{x}\text{ exterior normal to }\Sigma_{\epsilon}\,,

\displaystyle v_{1}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle:=u_{1}\big|_{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}\,.

Here, again, the minus sign in
w_{1}
arises because
\bm{n}_{x}
points into
\Omega_{\epsilon}
.
Then, dropping the subscripts and using (
24
) in (
25
), for a harmonic function
u
in
\Omega_{\epsilon}
, the Dirichlet and Neumann boundary data
v
and
w
, respectively, may be related by

(26)

\left(\textstyle\frac{1}{2}{\bf I}-\mathcal{D}\right)[v](\bm{x})=\mathcal{S}[w](\bm{x})\,,\quad\bm{x}\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}\,.

Using (
26
), the slender body Neumann data
f(s)
and Dirichlet data
v(s)
may be related via

(27)

\displaystyle\left(\textstyle\frac{1}{2}{\bf I}-\mathcal{D}\right)[v(s)]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}[w(s,\theta)]

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}w(s,\theta)\,\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}(s,\theta)\,d\theta

\displaystyle=f(s)\,.

Here we consider the Neumann boundary value
w(s,\theta)=w(\bm{x}(s,\theta))
as a function of the surface parameters
s
and
\theta
along
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
.

In the straight setting, we may use the representation (
27
) to exactly recover the symbol
m_{\epsilon}(k)
in (
13
). In particular, using
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
to denote the single and double layer operators defined along the straight filament
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 1.3

(Single and double layer operators on
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
)
.

Along the straight filament
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
, the behavior of the single and double layer operators
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
is given by explicit Fourier multipliers:

(28)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{S}}[e^{2\pi iks}e^{i\ell\theta}]

\displaystyle=m_{\mathcal{S}}(k,\ell)e^{2\pi iks}\cos(\ell\theta)\,,

\displaystyle m_{\mathcal{S}}(k,\ell)

\displaystyle=\epsilon\,I_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)K_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\,;

(29)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{D}}[e^{2\pi iks}e^{i\ell\theta}]

\displaystyle=m_{\mathcal{D}}(k,\ell)e^{2\pi iks}\cos(\ell\theta)\,,

\displaystyle m_{\mathcal{D}}(k,\ell)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}-2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert I_{0}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)K_{1}(\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\,,&\ell=0\\
\frac{1}{2}-2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert I_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\big(K_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert-1}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)+K_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert+1}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\big)\,,&\ell\neq 0\,.\end{cases}

where here each
I_{j}
and
K_{j}
denote modified Bessel functions of the first and second kind, respectively.

Furthermore, noting that by (
28
),
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
is invertible on the space of
\theta
-independent functions, we use
m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(k)
to denote

(30)

m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(k)=\frac{1}{m_{\mathcal{S}}(k,0)}=\frac{1}{\epsilon I_{0}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)K_{0}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)}\,.

Using the form (
30
) of
m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(k)
, we may show that
\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}
satisfies the following mapping properties.

Lemma 1.4

(Inverse single layer on
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
)
.

Given
g\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
with
\int_{\mathbb{T}}g(s)\,ds=0
, we have

(31)

\left\lVert\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}[g]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c\,\big(\epsilon^{-1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}+|g|_{\dot{C}^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\big)}\,.

The proofs of Lemmas
1.3
and
1.4
appear in section
2
.

Motivated by the explicit information for
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
, we consider the components of the relation (
27
) along the curved filament
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
as perturbations of the straight setting. Since the straight single layer operator
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
is only well defined for functions with zero mean in
s
, we will use
\mathbb{P}_{0}
to denote the projection
\mathbb{P}_{0}g(s,\theta)=g(s,\theta)-\int_{\mathbb{T}}g(s,\theta)\,ds
. We then write the map
v(s)\mapsto f(s)
as

(32)

\displaystyle\textstyle(\frac{1}{2}{\bf I}-\overline{\mathcal{D}}-\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}})[v(s)]

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathcal{S}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{P}_{0}}w(s,\theta)]+\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{P}_{0}}w(s,\theta)]+\mathcal{S}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}}w\,ds\bigg]

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}w(s,\theta)\,\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}(s,\theta)\,d\theta

\displaystyle=f(s)\,.

Here we are parameterizing
\bm{x}\in\Gamma_{\epsilon}
as
\bm{x}(s,\theta)=\bm{X}(s)+\epsilon\bm{e}_{r}(s,\theta)
, and we consider functions defined along
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
in terms of the surface parameters
s
and
\theta
. This will allow for direct comparison with the straight filament where
\overline{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
is parameterized by
\overline{\bm{x}}(s,\theta)=s\bm{e}_{z}+\epsilon\bm{e}_{r}(\theta)
.

Given surface densities
\varphi(s,\theta)
with
\int_{\mathbb{T}}\varphi(s,\theta)\,ds=0
and
\psi(s,\theta)
, the remainder terms
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}
may then be written as

(33)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}[\varphi]

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{S}-\overline{\mathcal{S}})[\varphi]=\int_{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}\big(\mathcal{G}(s,\theta,s^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})-\overline{\mathcal{G}}(s,\theta,s^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\big)\,\varphi(s^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\,\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\,d\theta^{\prime}ds^{\prime}

(34)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}[\psi]

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{D}-\overline{\mathcal{D}})[\psi]=\int_{\Gamma_{\epsilon}}\big(K_{\mathcal{D}}(s,\theta,s^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})-\overline{K}_{\mathcal{D}}(s,\theta,s^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\big)\,\psi(s^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\,\mathcal{J}_{\epsilon}(s^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\,d\theta^{\prime}ds^{\prime}\,.

Accordingly, we will consider the mapping properties of each of the operators in (
32
) in terms of
s
and
\theta
. In particular, for any
h\in C^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})
, we consider
h=h(s,\theta)
and understand the
\dot{C}^{0,\alpha}
seminorm (
10
) along
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
as

(35)

\left\lvert h\right\rvert_{\dot{C}^{0,\alpha}}=\sup_{(s-s^{\prime})^{2}+(\theta-\theta^{\prime})^{2}\neq 0}\frac{\left\lvert h(s,\theta)-h(s^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\right\rvert}{\sqrt{(s-s^{\prime})^{2}+\epsilon^{2}(\theta-\theta^{\prime})^{2}}^{\,\alpha}}\,.

Note that (
35
) and (
11
) can be seen to be equivalent characterizations of
\dot{C}^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})
using Lemma
3.1
. This characterization also helps make the
\epsilon
-dependence of the
\dot{C}^{0,\alpha}
seminorm along
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
more explicit.

Now, noting that
\overline{\mathcal{D}}[v(s)]
is independent of
\theta
and
\int_{0}^{2\pi}\overline{\mathcal{S}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{P}_{0}}w(s,\theta)]\,d\theta=\overline{\mathcal{S}}[\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{P}_{0}}w(s,\theta)\,d\theta]
, we may multiply the first line of (
32
) by
\epsilon
and integrate in
\theta
to obtain

(36)

\displaystyle\textstyle 2\pi\epsilon(\frac{1}{2}{\bf I}

\displaystyle-\overline{\mathcal{D}})[v(s)]-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}[v(s)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathcal{S}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{P}_{0}}w(s,\theta)\,\epsilon\,d\theta+\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{P}_{0}}w(s,\theta)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{S}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}}w\,ds\bigg]\,\epsilon\,d\theta}\,.

By Lemma
1.4
,
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
is invertible on the space of
\theta
-independent functions, and we may thus write

(37)

\displaystyle\textstyle\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}[v(s)]-\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}[v(s)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta-\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{P}_{0}}w(s,\theta)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\displaystyle-\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{S}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}}w\,ds\bigg]\,\epsilon\,d\theta}+\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}w(s,\theta)\,\epsilon\,dsd\theta-\epsilon^{2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}w(s,\theta)\widehat{\kappa}(s,\theta)\,d\theta=f(s)\,.

Here we have used that
f(s)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}w(s,\theta)\,\epsilon(1-\epsilon\widehat{\kappa})\,d\theta
, resulting in the final two terms on the left hand side.
We thus obtain a representation of the slender body DtN map
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}[v(s)]=f(s)
as a perturbation of the straight operator
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}[v(s)]
. Much of this paper will be devoted to showing that each of the remainder terms appearing in (
37
) are lower order with respect to regularity or size in
\epsilon
.

In particular, we show that the operators
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}
satisfy the following mapping properties:

Lemma 1.5

(Mapping properties of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}
)
.

Let
0<\alpha<\gamma<\beta<1
and let
\bm{X}\in C^{2,\beta}
be as in section
1.1
. Given
\varphi\in C^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})
with
\int_{\mathbb{T}}\varphi(s,\theta)\,ds=0
, the single layer remainder
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}
as in (
33
) may be decomposed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}[\varphi]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},\epsilon}[\varphi]+\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},+}[\varphi]

where

(38)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},\epsilon}[\varphi]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{2-\alpha}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}\,,\;\;\left\lvert\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},\epsilon}[\varphi]\right\rvert_{\dot{C}^{1,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}\leq c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},+}[\varphi]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-\alpha}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}\,,\;\;\left\lvert\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},+}[\varphi]\right\rvert_{\dot{C}^{1,\gamma}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}\leq c(\kappa_{*,\gamma},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-2\gamma}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}

for any
\alpha^{+}\in(\alpha,\beta]
.
Furthermore, given
\psi\in C^{0,\gamma}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})
, the double layer remainder
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}
as in (
34
) satisfies

(39)

\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}[\psi]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\gamma}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}\leq c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{+}}},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{+}}}\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}

for any
\gamma^{+}\in(\gamma,\beta]
.

The proof of Lemma
1.5
appears in section
3
. Note that the
\epsilon
-dependence in the bounds (
38
), (
39
) is explicit.

In addition to Lemma
1.5
, we will need lemma for dealing with the
\theta
-averaged single layer operator applied to a constant-in-
s
function on
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
.

Lemma 1.6

(Single layer applied to constant-in-
s
)
.

For
0<\alpha<\gamma\leq\beta<1
, let
\bm{X}\in C^{2,\beta}
be as in section
1.1
. Given
h(\theta)\in C^{0,\alpha}(2\pi\mathbb{T})
, we may write

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{S}[h(\theta)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta=\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}[h(\theta)]+\mathcal{H}_{+}[h(\theta)]

where

(40)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}[h]\right\rVert_{\cdot C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha^{+}},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{2-\alpha^{+}}\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(2\pi\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{+}[h]\right\rVert_{\cdot C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\gamma},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-\gamma}\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(2\pi\mathbb{T})}

for any
\alpha^{+}\in(\alpha,\beta]
.

The proof appears in section
3.4
.

Furthermore, using an alternative layer potential representation of the full Dirichlet-to-Neumann map, which we outline in section
4
, we may bound the full Neumann boundary value
w(s,\theta)
in terms of the Dirichlet data
v(s)
as follows:

Lemma 1.7

(Bound for
w(s,\theta)
)
.

Let
0<\alpha<\gamma<\beta<1
and consider
\bm{X}(s)\in C^{2,\beta}
as in section
1.1
.
Given
\theta
-independent Dirichlet data
v(s)\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
, consider the Neumann boundary value
w(s,\theta)
obtained from the full Dirichlet-to-Neumann map
v(s)\mapsto w(s,\theta)
along
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
. We have that
w(s,\theta)
may be decomposed as

\displaystyle w(s,\theta)=w_{0}(s,\theta)+w_{+}(s,\theta)

where

(41)

\displaystyle\left\lVert w_{0}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\alpha},c_{\Gamma})\,\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\left\lVert w_{+}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{+}}},c_{\Gamma},\epsilon)\,\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

Here the
\epsilon
-dependence is explicit in the bound for
w_{0}
but not for
w_{+}
.

The proof of Lemma
1.7
appears in section
4
.

Combining Lemmas
1.3
,
1.4
,
1.5
,
1.6
, and
1.7
, we arrive at the decomposition (
18
) of the slender body DtN map
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
stated in Theorem
1.2
(see section
1.3
for details). To obtain the decomposition (
20
) of the slender body NtD map
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
, we will additionally require the following lemma regarding a general bound for
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
in Hölder spaces.

Lemma 1.8

(Slender body NtD in Hölder spaces)
.

Given a curved slender body
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
as in section
1.1
with centerline
\bm{X}(s)\in C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
, and given slender body Neumann data
f(s)\in C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
with
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the slender body Neumann-to-Dirichlet map
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}[f]
(
27
) satisfies

(42)

\left\lVert\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}[f]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\leq c({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\kappa_{*,\alpha}},\epsilon)\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

The
\epsilon
-dependence in (
42
) is not explicit. The proof of Lemma
1.8
is given in section
5
.

In the next section, we proceed directly to showing how the key lemmas
1.3
,
1.4
,
1.5
,
1.6
,
1.7
, and
1.8
combine to yield Theorem
1.2
. In the remaining sections
2
,
3
,
4
, and
5
, we provide a proof for each of these key lemmas.

1.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.2

We first show the decomposition (
18
) for
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
. We begin by specifying the form of the remainder terms
\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}
. Using the decompositions of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}
and
w(s,\theta)
from Lemmas
1.5
and
1.7
, respectively, we may write

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon}[v(s)]=-\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},\epsilon}[\mathbb{P}_{0}w_{0}(s,\theta)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}}w_{0}\,ds\bigg]}-\epsilon^{2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}w_{0}(s,\theta)\widehat{\kappa}(s,\theta)\,d\theta

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}[v(s)]=-\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}[v(s)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta-\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},+}[\mathbb{P}_{0}w_{0}(s,\theta)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}}w_{+}\,ds\bigg]}

\displaystyle\quad-\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}[w_{+}(s,\theta)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\mathcal{H}_{+}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}}w\,ds\bigg]}-\epsilon^{2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}w_{+}(s,\theta)\widehat{\kappa}(s,\theta)\,d\theta+\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}w\,\epsilon\,dsd\theta\,.

To estimate
\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon}
, we begin by noting that, using Lemmas
1.5
and
1.7
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},\epsilon}[\mathbb{P}_{0}w_{0}(s,\theta)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi\epsilon\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},\epsilon}[\mathbb{P}_{0}w_{0}(s,\theta)]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{3-\alpha}\,c(\kappa_{*,\alpha},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert w_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}\leq\epsilon^{3-\alpha}\,c(\kappa_{*,\alpha},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},\epsilon}[\mathbb{P}_{0}w_{0}(s,\theta)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta\right\rvert_{\dot{C}^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},\epsilon}[\mathbb{P}_{0}w_{0}(s,\theta)]\right\rvert_{\dot{C}^{1,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{2-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}}\,c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert w_{0}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}\leq\epsilon^{2-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}}\,c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

Using Lemmas
1.4
and
1.6
, we may then estimate:

(43)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon}[v(s)]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{2}\,c(\kappa_{*,\alpha})\left\lVert w_{0}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}}w_{0}\,ds\bigg]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}}

\displaystyle+\left\lVert\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},\epsilon}[\mathbb{P}_{0}w_{0}(s,\theta)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}},c_{\Gamma})\bigg(\epsilon^{2}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}+\epsilon^{2-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}}\left\lVert w_{0}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}+\epsilon^{2-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{2-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}}\,c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

We next estimate
\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}[v(s)]
. We first note that, again using Lemmas
1.5
and
1.7
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}[v(s)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta\right\rVert_{C^{1,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{+}}},c_{\Gamma})\epsilon^{1-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{+}}}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}[\mathbb{P}_{0}w_{+}(s,\theta)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta\right\rVert_{C^{1,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{+}}},c_{\Gamma})\epsilon^{2-2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{+}}}\left\lVert w_{+}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}\leq c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{+}}},c_{\Gamma},\epsilon)\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},+}[\mathbb{P}_{0}w_{0}(s,\theta)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta\right\rVert_{C^{1,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,\gamma},c_{\Gamma})\epsilon^{2-2\gamma}\left\lVert w_{0}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}\leq c(\kappa_{*,\gamma},c_{\Gamma})\epsilon^{2-2\gamma}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

The
\epsilon
-dependence is explicit in the first and third bounds but not the second.
Using the above bounds along with Lemmas
1.4
,
1.6
, and
1.7
, we may estimate

(44)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}[v(s)]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{D}}[v(s)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}+\left\lVert\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S}}[\mathbb{P}_{0}w_{+}(s,\theta)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle+\left\lVert\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{S},+}[\mathbb{P}_{0}w_{0}(s,\theta)]\,\epsilon\,d\theta\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}}w_{+}\,ds\bigg]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\displaystyle+\left\lVert\mathcal{H}_{+}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}}w\,ds\bigg]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}}+\epsilon^{2}c(\kappa_{*,\gamma})\left\lVert w_{+}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}+\epsilon\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{+}}},c_{\Gamma},\epsilon)\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}+c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{+}}},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{2-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{+}}}\left\lVert w_{+}\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}

\displaystyle+c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{+}}},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{1-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{+}}}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\Gamma_{\epsilon})}+c(\kappa_{*,\gamma},c_{\Gamma})\,\epsilon^{2-2\gamma}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{+}}},c_{\Gamma},\epsilon)\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

Thus we obtain the estimates (
19
) for the decomposition (
18
) of
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
.

We next turn to the decomposition (
20
) for
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
. For
f(s)
satisfying
\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(s)\,ds=0
, we may use the
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
decomposition (
18
) to write

\displaystyle v(s)

\displaystyle=({\bf I}+\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{P}_{0}\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon})^{-1}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}[f(s)]-({\bf I}+\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{P}_{0}\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon})^{-1}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{P}_{0}\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}[v(s)]

\displaystyle=({\bf I}+\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{P}_{0}\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon})^{-1}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}[f(s)]-({\bf I}+\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{P}_{0}\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon})^{-1}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{P}_{0}\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}[f(s)]\,.

Note that, using Lemma
1.1
and the estimate (
43
) for
\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon}
, for any
g\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{P}_{0}\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon}[g]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq c\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{1/2}\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon}[g]\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}+c\epsilon^{-1}\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{1/2}\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon}[g]\right\rVert_{\dot{C}^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{1-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}}\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{1/2}\,c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

For
\epsilon
sufficiently small, we may use a Neumann series to write

(45)

({\bf I}+\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{P}_{0}\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon})^{-1}={\bf I}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(-\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{P}_{0}\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon})^{j}\;=:\;{\bf I}+\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,\epsilon}

where

(46)

\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,\epsilon}[g]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\epsilon^{1-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}}\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{1/2}\,c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{+}}},c_{\Gamma})\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

Next, writing

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,+}=-({\bf I}+\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{P}_{0}\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,\epsilon})^{-1}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{P}_{0}\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}\,,

we may use (
45
) and (
46
) along with Lemma
1.1
, estimate (
44
), and Lemma
1.8
to obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,+}[f(s)]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\gamma}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert({\bf I}+\mathcal{R}_{\rm n,\epsilon})\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{P}_{0}\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}[f(s)]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\gamma}}\leq c(\kappa_{*,\beta},c_{\Gamma},\epsilon)\left\lVert\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}\mathbb{P}_{0}\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}[f(s)]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\gamma}}

\displaystyle\leq c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{+}}},c_{\Gamma},\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\rm d,+}\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}[f(s)]\right\rVert_{C^{0,\gamma}}\leq c(\kappa_{*,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{+}}},c_{\Gamma},\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}[f(s)]\right\rVert_{C^{1,\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq c({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\kappa_{*,\gamma^{+}}},c_{\Gamma},\epsilon)\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})}\,.

Altogether, we obtain the decomposition (
20
) and bounds (
21
) for
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
.
∎

The remainder of the paper is structured as follows. Section
2
contains the proofs of Lemmas
1.1
,
1.3
, and
1.4
regarding mapping properties in the straight setting; section
3
contains the proof of the layer potential remainder estimates in Lemmas
1.5
and
1.6
; section
4
gives the proof of Lemma
1.7
bounding the full Neumann boundary value
w(s,\theta)
; and section
5
has the proof of Hölder space estimates for
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
from Lemma
1.8
.

2.
Mapping properties in the straight setting

In this section we prove mapping properties for the slender body NtD and its components about the straight cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
.

2.1.
Symbols for single and double layer

We begin with Lemma
1.3
regarding explicit expressions for the single and double layer operators
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
on
\mathcal{C}_{\epsilon}
.

Note that, due to the form of the kernels (
48
), periodicity in
s
will be enforced by considering only densities
\varphi
which are 1-periodic in
s
. We may then write the operators
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
as

(47)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{S}}[\varphi](s,\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\overline{\mathcal{G}}(s,\theta,s^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\,\varphi(s^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\,\epsilon\,d\theta^{\prime}ds^{\prime}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{D}}[\varphi](s,\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\overline{K}_{\mathcal{D}}(s,\theta,s^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\,\varphi(s^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\,\epsilon\,d\theta^{\prime}ds^{\prime}

where

(48)

\overline{\mathcal{G}}(s,\theta,s^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(s-s^{\prime})^{2}+4\epsilon^{2}\sin^{2}(\frac{\theta-\theta^{\prime}}{2})}}\,,\;\;\overline{K}_{\mathcal{D}}(s,\theta,s^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})=\frac{-2\epsilon\sin^{2}(\frac{\theta-\theta^{\prime}}{2})}{\sqrt{(s-s^{\prime})^{2}+4\epsilon^{2}\sin^{2}(\frac{\theta-\theta^{\prime}}{2})}^{\,3}}\,.

Note that in the straight setting, both
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
and
\overline{K}_{\mathcal{D}}
are convolution kernels in both
s
and
\theta
.

We use the expressions (
47
), (
48
) to derive the symbols for
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
given by Lemma
1.3
.

Proof of Lemma
1.3
.

For
k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
, we may calculate an exact expression for
\overline{\mathcal{S}}[e^{2\pi iks}e^{i\ell\theta}]
. We begin with explicit integration in
s
:

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{S}}[e^{2\pi iks}e^{i\ell\theta}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{1}{\sqrt{(s^{\prime})^{2}+4\epsilon^{2}\sin^{2}(\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2})}}e^{2\pi ik(s-s^{\prime})}e^{i\ell(\theta-\theta^{\prime})}\,\epsilon d\theta^{\prime}ds^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}K_{0}(4\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert\textstyle\sin(\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2}))\,e^{-i\ell\theta^{\prime}}\,\epsilon d\theta^{\prime}\;e^{2\pi iks}e^{i\ell\theta}\,.

Now, due to symmetry in
\theta^{\prime}
, we have that for
z\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and any integer
\ell\neq 0
,

(49)

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\sin(\ell\theta^{\prime})K_{0}(z\textstyle\sin(\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2}))\,d\theta^{\prime}=0\,.

Furthermore, using the definition of the zeroth order modified Bessel function
K_{0}
, we may write

(50)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos(\ell\theta^{\prime})K_{0}(z\textstyle\sin(\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2}))\,d\theta^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(\ell\theta^{\prime})\frac{\cos(zt\,\sin(\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2}))}{\sqrt{t^{2}+1}}\,dt\,d\theta^{\prime}

\displaystyle=2\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{J_{2\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert}(zt)}{\sqrt{t^{2}+1}}\,dt

\displaystyle=2\pi\textstyle I_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert}(\frac{z}{2})K_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert}(\frac{z}{2})\,,

where
J_{2\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert}
and
I_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert}
are, respectively, Bessel functions and modified Bessel functions of the first kind.
Altogether, we find that
\overline{\mathcal{S}}[e^{2\pi iks}e^{i\ell\theta}]
may be explicitly evaluated to obtain

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{S}}[e^{2\pi iks}e^{i\ell\theta}]

\displaystyle=\epsilon\,I_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)K_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\;e^{2\pi iks}\cos(\ell\theta)\,.

For the double layer potential
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
, we may calculate

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{D}}[e^{2\pi iks}e^{i\ell\theta}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{-2\epsilon\sin^{2}(\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2})}{\sqrt{(s^{\prime})^{2}+4\epsilon^{2}\sin^{2}(\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2})}^{\,3}}\,e^{2\pi ik(s-s^{\prime})}e^{i\ell(\theta-\theta^{\prime})}\,\epsilon d\theta^{\prime}ds^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{2\pi}\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert|\textstyle\sin(\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2})|\,K_{1}\big(4\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert|\textstyle\sin(\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2})|\big)\,e^{-i\ell\theta^{\prime}}\,d\theta^{\prime}\;e^{2\pi iks}e^{i\ell\theta}\,.

For
z>0
, we have

\displaystyle-\textstyle z|\sin(\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2})|K_{1}\big(z|\sin(\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2})|\big)=z\partial_{z}K_{0}\big(z|\sin(\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2})|\big)\,,

and, using (
49
) and (
50
) from above, we have

\displaystyle z\partial_{z}\int_{0}^{2\pi}

\displaystyle\textstyle K_{0}\big(z|\sin(\frac{\theta^{\prime}}{2})|\big)\,e^{-i\ell\theta^{\prime}}\,d\theta^{\prime}=\textstyle 2\pi z\partial_{z}\big(I_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert}(\frac{z}{2})K_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert}(\frac{z}{2})\big)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\pi z\textstyle\big(I_{1}(\frac{z}{2})K_{0}(\frac{z}{2})-I_{0}(\frac{z}{2})K_{1}(\frac{z}{2})\big)\,,&\ell=0\\
\pi z\textstyle\left[\big(I_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert-1}(\frac{z}{2})+I_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert+1}(\frac{z}{2})\big)K_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert}(\frac{z}{2})-I_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert}(\frac{z}{2})\big(K_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert-1}(\frac{z}{2})+K_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert+1}(\frac{z}{2})\big)\right]\,,&\ell\neq 0\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\textstyle 2\pi-2\pi zI_{0}(\frac{z}{2})K_{1}(\frac{z}{2})\,,&\ell=0\\
\textstyle 2\pi-2\pi zI_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert}(\frac{z}{2})\big(K_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert-1}(\frac{z}{2})+K_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert+1}(\frac{z}{2})\big)\,,&\ell\neq 0\,,\end{cases}

where we have used the identity

\displaystyle I_{j+1}(z)K_{j}(z)+I_{j}(z)K_{j+1}(z)=\frac{1}{z}\,.

Taking
z=4\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert
, we then have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{D}}[e^{2\pi iks}e^{i\ell\theta}]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}-2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert I_{0}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)K_{1}(\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\bigg)\,e^{2\pi iks}\,,&\ell=0\\
\bigg(\frac{1}{2}-2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert I_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\big(K_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert-1}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)+K_{\left\lvert\ell\right\rvert+1}(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert k\right\rvert)\big)\bigg)e^{2\pi iks}\cos(\ell\theta)\,,&\ell\neq 0\,.\end{cases}

∎

2.2.
Mapping properties: preliminaries

We next turn to the proofs of Lemmas
1.1
and
1.4
regarding mapping properties of the operators
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
and
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
in the straight setting. We will begin by building up some background tools.

We consider
C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
as a subset of
C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{R})
. The proof will rely on the characterization of
C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{R})
as the Besov space
B^{\alpha}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
(see
[
4
, Theorem 2.36]
).
Consider a smooth cutoff function
\phi(\xi)
supported on the annulus
\{\frac{3}{4}<\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert<2\}
and satisfying
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\phi(2^{-j}\xi)=1
. Let

(51)

\phi_{j}(\xi)=\phi(2^{-j}\xi)

and, given a function
g(s)
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[g](\xi)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}g(s)\,e^{-2\pi i\xi s}\,ds

denote the Fourier transform of
g
. Define the Littlewood-Paley projection
P_{j}g
onto frequencies supported in annulus
j
by

(52)

\mathcal{F}[P_{j}g](\xi)=\phi_{j}(\xi)\mathcal{F}[g](\xi)\,.

We also define

\displaystyle P_{\leq j_{*}}:=\sum_{j\leq j_{*}}P_{j}\,.

For
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
, we may then define the homogeneous Besov seminorm

\displaystyle|g|_{\dot{B}^{\sigma}_{\infty,\infty}}=\sup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{j\sigma}\left\lVert P_{j}g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

as well as the inhomogeneous norm

(53)

\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{B^{\sigma}_{\infty,\infty}}=\sup\big(\left\lVert P_{\leq 0}\,g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}},\sup_{j>0}2^{j\sigma}\left\lVert P_{j}g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\big)\,.

Letting
\lfloor\sigma\rfloor
denote the integer part of
\sigma
, we have that the
B^{\sigma}_{\infty,\infty}
norm (
53
) is equivalent to the
C^{\lfloor\sigma\rfloor,\sigma-\lfloor\sigma\rfloor}
Hölder norm (
11
).

Given a Fourier multiplier
m(\xi)
and a function
g(s)
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we will use the notation

\displaystyle T_{m}g

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{-1}[m\mathcal{F}[g]]\,,\qquad P_{j}T_{m}g=\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\phi_{j}m\mathcal{F}[g]]\,.

To obtain the mapping properties of
T_{m}g
, we will need to measure the components
P_{j}T_{m}g
of the norm
\left\lVert T_{m}g\right\rVert_{B^{\sigma}_{\infty,\infty}}
given by (
53
). Let
M_{j}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\phi_{j}m],
so
P_{j}T_{m}g=M_{j}*g
. Then by Young’s inequality for convolutions we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert P_{j}T_{m}g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}=\left\lVert M_{j}*g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\left\lVert M_{j}\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\,.

The general strategy will thus be to obtain
L^{1}
bounds for the functions
M_{j}
in physical space. The following lemma will be useful.

Lemma 2.1

(Physical space
L^{1}
bounds for multipliers)
.

For fixed
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, suppose that we have a Fourier multiplier
\widehat{M}_{j}(\xi)\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
supported in the annulus
2^{j-1}<\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert<2^{j+1}
and satisfying the bounds

(54)

\left\lvert\widehat{M}_{j}\right\rvert\leq A\,,\qquad\left\lvert\partial_{\xi}^{2}\widehat{M}_{j}\right\rvert\leq B

for some numbers
A,B
.
Then on the physical side
M_{j}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\widehat{M}_{j}]
satisfies the
L^{1}
estimate

(55)

\left\lVert M_{j}\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim 2^{j}\sqrt{AB}\,.

Proof.

Integrating the
L^{\infty}
bounds (
54
) over the support of
\widehat{M}_{j}
, we obtain the
L^{1}
bounds

\displaystyle\left\lVert\widehat{M}_{j}\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim 2^{j}A\,,\qquad\left\lVert\partial_{\xi}^{2}\widehat{M}_{j}\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim 2^{j}B\,,

which imply the physical space estimates

\displaystyle\left\lVert M_{j}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim 2^{j}A\,,\qquad\left\lVert s^{2}M_{j}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{j}B\,.

Using the above
L^{\infty}
bounds, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\lvert M_{j}\right\rvert\,ds=\int_{\left\lvert s\right\rvert\leq R}\left\lvert M_{j}\right\rvert\,ds+\int_{\left\lvert s\right\rvert>R}\left\lvert M_{j}\right\rvert\,ds

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{j}RA+2^{j}\frac{B}{R}

for some
R>0
. Equating
RA=\frac{B}{R}
, we find
R=\sqrt{\frac{B}{A}}
, from which we obtain (
55
).
∎

The remainder of this section will be devoted to showing bounds of the form (
54
) for our multipliers
m_{\epsilon}
,
m_{\epsilon}^{-1}
, and
m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}
.

2.3.
Multiplier estimates

Given the expressions (
13
) and (
30
) for our multipliers, we first state some general bounds for modified Bessel functions.

Proposition 2.2

(Modified Bessel function bounds)
.

Let
K_{j}(z)
denote the
j
-th order modified Bessel function of the second kind. For some constant
c>0
, we have

(56)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{K_{1}(z)}{K_{0}(z)}-1-\frac{1}{2z}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{z^{2}}\,,\quad\left\lvert\frac{K_{0}(z)}{K_{1}(z)}-1+\frac{1}{2z}\right\rvert\leq\frac{c}{z^{2}}\,,\qquad z\geq 1\,,

(57)

\displaystyle\frac{c_{1}}{\left\lvert\log(z)\right\rvert}

\displaystyle<z\frac{K_{1}(z)}{K_{0}(z)}<\frac{c_{2}}{\left\lvert\log(z)\right\rvert}\,,\qquad 0<z<1\,.

Furthermore, for
I_{j}(z)
, the
j
-th order modified Bessel function of the first kind, we have

(58)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{I_{1}(z)}{I_{0}(z)}-1+\frac{1}{2z}\right\rvert\leq\frac{c}{z^{2}}\,,\qquad z\geq 1\,,

(59)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{I_{1}(z)}{I_{0}(z)}-\frac{z}{2}\right\rvert\leq c\,z^{3}\,,\qquad 0<z<1\,.

The bounds of Proposition
2.2
follow directly from well-documented large-
z
and small-
z
asymptotics of
K_{0}(z)
,
K_{1}(z)
,
I_{0}(z)
, and
I_{1}(z)
(see
[
14
, Chapter 10]
).

Using Proposition
2.2
, we may prove the following bounds for the multipliers
m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}
,
m_{\epsilon}
, and
m_{\epsilon}^{-1}
.

Lemma 2.3

(Bounds for multipliers)
.

The inverse single layer multiplier
m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(\xi)
given by (
30
) satisfies the bounds

(60)

\left\lvert\partial_{\xi}^{\ell}m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(\xi)\right\rvert\leq\begin{cases}c\,\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{1-\ell}\,,&\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert\geq\frac{1}{2\pi\epsilon}\\
c\,\epsilon^{-1}\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{-\ell}\,,&\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert<\frac{1}{2\pi\epsilon}\,,\end{cases}\ell=0,1,2\,.

Furthermore, for the DtN multiplier
m_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\xi)
as in (
13
), we have that

(61)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\partial_{\xi}^{\ell}m_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\xi)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq c\,\epsilon\,\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{1-\ell}\,,\;\ell=0,1,2\,,\qquad\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert\geq\frac{1}{2\pi\epsilon}\,,

\displaystyle\left\lvert m_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\xi)\right\rvert\leq\frac{c}{\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert}\,,\quad\left\lvert\partial_{\xi}^{\ell}m_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\xi)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{\ell}\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{2}}\,,\;\ell=1,2\,,\qquad\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert<\frac{1}{2\pi\epsilon}\,.

For the NtD multiplier
m_{\epsilon}(\xi)=(m_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\xi))^{-1}
, we have

(62)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\partial_{\xi}^{\ell}m_{\epsilon}(\xi)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\epsilon\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{\ell+1}}\,,\;\ell=0,1,2\,,\qquad\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert\geq\frac{1}{2\pi\epsilon}

\displaystyle\left\lvert m_{\epsilon}(\xi)\right\rvert\leq c\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert\,,\quad\left\lvert\partial_{\xi}^{\ell}m_{\epsilon}(\xi)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^{\ell}}\,,\qquad\ell=1,2\,,\qquad\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert<\frac{1}{2\pi\epsilon}\,.

Proof of Lemma
2.3
.

We begin with the bounds (
60
) for
m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}
. Define

\displaystyle g(z):=\frac{1}{I_{0}(z)K_{0}(z)}

and note that
g(z)>0
for all
z>0
.
Using Lemma
2.2
, we have that
g^{\prime}(z)\geq 0
satisfies

(63)

g^{\prime}(z)=g(z)\bigg(\frac{K_{1}(z)}{K_{0}(z)}-\frac{I_{1}(z)}{I_{0}(z)}\bigg)\leq g(z)\bigg(\frac{1}{z}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{c}{z^{2}}}\bigg)

for
z\geq 1
. By Grönwall’s inequality, we then have

(64)

g(z)\leq g(1)\,{\rm exp}\bigg(\int_{1}^{z}\bigg(\frac{1}{s}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\frac{c}{s^{2}}}\bigg)\,ds\bigg)=c\,e^{\log(z){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+c(1-\frac{1}{z})}}\leq c\,z\,,\qquad z\geq 1.

For
0<z<1
, we simply note that since
g
is monotone increasing,
g(z)\leq g(1)
.

Using (
64
) in (
63
), we also obtain

(65)

g^{\prime}(z)\leq\begin{cases}c\,,&z\geq 1\\
\frac{c}{z}\,,&z<1\,.\end{cases}

In addition, we may calculate

\displaystyle\left\lvert g^{\prime\prime}(z)\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\bigg(g^{\prime}(z)-\frac{g(z)}{z}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{K_{1}(z)}{K_{0}(z)}-\frac{I_{1}(z)}{I_{0}(z)}\bigg)+g(z)\bigg(\frac{K_{1}^{2}(z)}{K_{0}^{2}(z)}+\frac{I_{1}^{2}(z)}{I_{0}^{2}(z)}-2\bigg)\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\bigg(g^{\prime}-\frac{g}{z}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}-\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}\bigg)+g\bigg(\bigg(\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}-1\bigg)^{2}+\bigg(\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}-1\bigg)^{2}+2\bigg(\frac{K_{1}}{K_{0}}+\frac{I_{1}}{I_{0}}-2\bigg)\bigg)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{z}\left\lvert g^{\prime}-\frac{g}{z}\right\rvert+c\frac{\left\lvert g\right\rvert}{z^{2}}\,,

by Lemma
2.2
. Using the bounds (
64
) and (
65
) for
g
and
g^{\prime}
, we then have

(66)

\left\lvert g^{\prime\prime}(z)\right\rvert\leq\begin{cases}\frac{c}{z}\,,&z\geq 1\\
\frac{c}{z^{2}}\,,&z<1\,.\end{cases}

Using that

\displaystyle m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(\xi)=\epsilon^{-1}g(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert)\,,

with each of (
64
), (
65
), and (
66
), we obtain the bounds (
60
).

We next turn to the bounds (
61
). Let
H(z)=z\frac{K_{1}(z)}{K_{0}(z)}
. By Proposition
2.2
, for
z\geq 1
we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert H(z)-z-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{z}

\displaystyle\left\lvert H^{\prime}(z)-1\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\frac{1}{z}(H+z)(H-z)-1\right\rvert=\left\lvert 2(H-z)+\frac{1}{z}(H-z)^{2}-1\right\rvert\leq\frac{c}{z}

\displaystyle\left\lvert H^{\prime\prime}(z)\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert-\frac{1}{z^{2}}(H+z)(H-z)+\frac{1}{z}(H^{\prime}+1)(H-z)+\frac{1}{z}(H+z)(H^{\prime}-1)\right\rvert\leq\frac{c}{z}\,.

Furthermore, for
z<1
we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert H(z)\right\rvert\leq\frac{c}{\left\lvert\log(z)\right\rvert}\,,\quad\left\lvert H^{\prime}(z)\right\rvert\leq\frac{c}{z\log^{2}(z)}\,,\quad\left\lvert H^{\prime\prime}(z)\right\rvert\leq\frac{c}{z^{2}\log^{2}(z)}\,.

Thus, using that

\displaystyle m_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\xi)=2\pi H(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert)\,,

we obtain the bounds (
61
).

We next consider
G(z)=\frac{1}{H(z)}=\frac{K_{0}(z)}{zK_{1}(z)}
. For
z\geq 1
, using Proposition
2.2
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert G(z)-\frac{1}{z}+\frac{1}{2z^{2}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{z^{3}}

\displaystyle\left\lvert G^{\prime}(z)+\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert z\bigg(G(z)-\frac{1}{z}\bigg)^{2}+z\bigg(G(z)+\frac{1}{z}\bigg)\bigg(G(z)-\frac{1}{z}+\frac{1}{2z^{2}}\bigg)\right\rvert\leq\frac{c}{z^{3}}

\displaystyle\left\lvert G^{\prime\prime}(z)\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\bigg(G+\frac{1}{z}\bigg)\bigg(G-\frac{1}{z}\bigg)+z\bigg(G^{\prime}-\frac{1}{z^{2}}\bigg)\bigg(G-\frac{1}{z}\bigg)+z\bigg(G+\frac{1}{z}\bigg)\bigg(G^{\prime}+\frac{1}{z^{2}}\bigg)\right\rvert\leq\frac{c}{z^{3}}\,.

For
z<1
, we have

\displaystyle G(z)<c\left\lvert\log(z)\right\rvert\,.

By the above forms of
G^{\prime}(z)
and
G^{\prime\prime}(z)
, we may then obtain the bounds

\displaystyle\left\lvert G^{\prime}(z)\right\rvert\leq\frac{c}{z}\,,\qquad\left\lvert G^{\prime\prime}(z)\right\rvert\leq\frac{c}{z^{2}}\,,\qquad 0<z<1\,.

Using that

\displaystyle m_{\epsilon}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi}G(2\pi\epsilon\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert)\,,

we obtain the estimates (
62
).
∎

2.4.
Proof of Lemmas
1.1
and
1.4

Using the multiplier estimates of Lemma
2.3
, we may finally prove Lemmas
1.1
and
1.4
regarding the mapping properties of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
,
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
, and
\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}
.

We begin with the mapping properties of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}
.

Proof of Lemma
1.1
.

We first define the Fourier multipliers
\widehat{M}^{(1)}_{\epsilon,j}(\xi)=\phi_{j}(\xi)m_{\epsilon}(\xi)
and
\widehat{M}^{(2)}_{\epsilon,j}(\xi)=\phi_{j}(\xi)m_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\xi)
for
\phi_{j}
as in (
51
). For
j_{\epsilon}=\frac{\left\lvert\log(2\pi\epsilon)\right\rvert}{\log(2)}
, we may use Lemma
2.3
to calculate

\displaystyle\left\lVert\partial_{\xi}^{\ell}\widehat{M}^{(1)}_{\epsilon,j}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}c\,\epsilon^{-1}2^{-j(\ell+1)}\,,&j\geq j_{\epsilon}\,,\;\ell=0,1,2\\
c\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert\,,&j<j_{\epsilon}\,,\;\ell=0\\
c\,2^{-j\ell}\,,&j<j_{\epsilon}\,,\;\ell=1,2\,.\end{cases}

as well as

\displaystyle\left\lVert\partial_{\xi}^{\ell}\widehat{M}^{(2)}_{\epsilon,j}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq\begin{cases}c\,\epsilon\,2^{j(1-\ell)}\,,&j\geq j_{\epsilon}\\
c\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{-1}2^{-j\ell}\,,&j<j_{\epsilon}\,,\end{cases}\quad\ell=0,1,2\,.

By Lemma
2.1
, we thus obtain the following physical space estimates for
M^{(1)}_{\epsilon,j}
and
M^{(2)}_{\epsilon,j}
:

\displaystyle\left\lVert M^{(1)}_{\epsilon,j}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\leq\begin{cases}c\,\epsilon^{-1}2^{-j}\,,&j\geq j_{\epsilon}\\
c\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{1/2}\,,&j<j_{\epsilon}\,;\end{cases}\qquad\left\lVert M^{(2)}_{\epsilon,j}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\leq\begin{cases}c\,\epsilon\,2^{j}\,,&j\geq j_{\epsilon}\\
c\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{-1}\,,&j<j_{\epsilon}\,.\end{cases}

Now, given
h\in C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
with
\int_{\mathbb{T}}h(s)\,ds=0
and
g\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
, we use the dyadic partition of unity (
51
) to write

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}[h]=T_{m_{\epsilon}}h=\sum_{j}P_{j}T_{m_{\epsilon}}h\,;\qquad\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}^{-1}[g]=T_{m_{\epsilon}^{-1}}g=\sum_{j}P_{j}T_{m_{\epsilon}^{-1}}g\,.

Noting that on
\mathbb{T}
,

P_{\leq 0}T_{m_{\epsilon}}h=P_{0}T_{m_{\epsilon}}h
and
P_{\leq 0}T_{m_{\epsilon}^{-1}}g=P_{0}T_{m_{\epsilon}^{-1}}g
, using the above
L^{1}
bounds we may estimate

(67)

\displaystyle\left\lVert P_{\leq 0}T_{m_{\epsilon}}h\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert M^{(1)}_{\epsilon,0}*h\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq\left\lVert M^{(1)}_{\epsilon,0}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq c\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{1/2}\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\,;

\displaystyle\left\lVert P_{\leq 0}T_{m_{\epsilon}^{-1}}g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert M^{(2)}_{\epsilon,0}*g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq\left\lVert M^{(2)}_{\epsilon,0}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq c\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{-1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\,.

For
j>0
, we will introduce the notation
\widetilde{P}_{j}=P_{j-1}+P_{j}+P_{j+1}
. Note that, due to the support of
\mathcal{F}[P_{j}\,\cdot]
(
52
), we have
P_{j}T_{m_{\epsilon}}h=P_{j}T_{m_{\epsilon}}\widetilde{P}_{j}h
and
P_{j}T_{m_{\epsilon}^{-1}}g=P_{j}T_{m_{\epsilon}^{-1}}\widetilde{P}_{j}g
.
We estimate the low frequencies
0<j<j_{\epsilon}
of
T_{m_{\epsilon}}h
and
T_{m_{\epsilon}^{-1}}g
via

(68)

\displaystyle\sup_{0<j<j_{\epsilon}}2^{j(1+\alpha)}\left\lVert P_{j}T_{m_{\epsilon}}h\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{0<j<j_{\epsilon}}2^{j(1+\alpha)}\left\lVert P_{j}T_{m_{\epsilon}}\widetilde{P}_{j}h\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{0<j<j_{\epsilon}}2^{j(1+\alpha)}\left\lVert M^{(1)}_{\epsilon,j}*(\widetilde{P}_{j}h)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{j_{\epsilon}}\sup_{0<j<j_{\epsilon}}\left\lVert M^{(1)}_{\epsilon,j}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}2^{j\alpha}\left\lVert\widetilde{P}_{j}h\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq c\,\epsilon^{-1}\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{1/2}\left\lvert h\right\rvert_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty,\infty}}\,;

\displaystyle\sup_{0<j<j_{\epsilon}}2^{j\alpha}\left\lVert P_{j}T_{m_{\epsilon}^{-1}}g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{0<j<j_{\epsilon}}2^{j\alpha}\left\lVert P_{j}T_{m_{\epsilon}^{-1}}\widetilde{P}_{j}g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}=\sup_{0<j<j_{\epsilon}}2^{j\alpha}\left\lVert M^{(2)}_{\epsilon,j}*(\widetilde{P}_{j}g)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{0<j<j_{\epsilon}}\left\lVert M^{(2)}_{\epsilon,j}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}2^{j\alpha}\left\lVert\widetilde{P}_{j}g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq c\,\left\lvert\log\epsilon\right\rvert^{-1}\left\lvert g\right\rvert_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty,\infty}}\,.

For high frequencies
j\geq j_{\epsilon}
, we may estimate

(69)

\displaystyle\sup_{j\geq j_{\epsilon}}2^{j(1+\alpha)}\left\lVert P_{j}T_{m_{\epsilon}}h\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{j\geq j_{\epsilon}}2^{j(1+\alpha)}\left\lVert M^{(1)}_{\epsilon,j}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\left\lVert\widetilde{P}_{j}h\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq c\,\epsilon^{-1}\sup_{j\geq j_{\epsilon}}\,2^{j\alpha}\|\widetilde{P}_{j}h\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq c\,\epsilon^{-1}\left\lvert h\right\rvert_{\dot{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty,\infty}}\,;

\displaystyle\sup_{j\geq j_{\epsilon}}2^{j\alpha}\left\lVert P_{j}T_{m_{\epsilon}^{-1}}g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{j\geq j_{\epsilon}}2^{j\alpha}\left\lVert M^{(2)}_{\epsilon,j}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\left\lVert\widetilde{P}_{j}g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq c\,\epsilon\sup_{j\geq j_{\epsilon}}\,2^{j(1+\alpha)}\|\widetilde{P}_{j}g\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq c\,\epsilon\left\lvert g\right\rvert_{\dot{B}^{1+\alpha}_{\infty,\infty}}\,.

Combining the estimates (
67
), (
68
), and (
69
) and using the Besov characterization (
53
) of
C^{k,\alpha}(\mathbb{R})
, we obtain the mapping properties (
16
) and (
17
) of Lemma
1.1
.
∎

Finally, we show Lemma
1.4
regarding the operator
\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}
.

Proof of Lemma
1.4
.

Define the Fourier multiplier
\widehat{M}_{\mathcal{S},j}(\xi)=\phi_{j}(\xi)m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(\xi)
where
\phi_{j}
is given by (
51
). Using the estimates (
60
) for
m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}
from Lemma
2.3
and taking
j_{\epsilon}=\frac{\left\lvert\log(2\pi\epsilon)\right\rvert}{\log(2)}
, we see that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\partial_{\xi}^{\ell}\widehat{M}_{\mathcal{S},j}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}c\,2^{j(1-\ell)}\,,&j\geq j_{\epsilon}\\
c\,\epsilon^{-1}2^{-j\ell}\,,&j<j_{\epsilon}\,,\end{cases}\qquad\ell=0,1,2\,.

Using Lemma
2.1
, we then have the following physical space estimate for
M_{\mathcal{S},j}
:

\displaystyle\left\lVert M_{\mathcal{S},j}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\leq\begin{cases}c\,2^{j}\,,&j\geq j_{\epsilon}\\
c\,\epsilon^{-1}\,,&j<j_{\epsilon}\,.\end{cases}

For
g\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
, we use the dyadic partition of unity (
51
) to write

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}[g]=T_{m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}}g=\sum_{j}P_{j}T_{m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}}g\,.

Noting again that
P_{\leq 0}T_{m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}}g=P_{0}T_{m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}}g
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert P_{\leq 0}T_{m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}}g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert M_{\mathcal{S},0}*g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq c\left\lVert M_{\mathcal{S},0}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq c\,\epsilon^{-1}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\,.

Again letting
\widetilde{P}_{j}g=(P_{j-1}+P_{j}+P_{j+1})g
and noting that
P_{j}T_{m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}}g=P_{j}T_{m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}}\widetilde{P}_{j}g
, for low frequencies
0<j<j_{\epsilon}
, we may estimate

\displaystyle\sup_{0<j<j_{\epsilon}}2^{j\alpha}\left\lVert P_{j}T_{m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}}g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{0<j<j_{\epsilon}}2^{j\alpha}\left\lVert P_{j}T_{m_{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}}\widetilde{P}_{j}g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}=\sup_{0<j<j_{\epsilon}}2^{j\alpha}\left\lVert M_{\mathcal{S},j}*(\widetilde{P}_{j}g)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}
